Abstract (英文): We investigate structural conditions under which preferred and stable semantics agree in abstract argumentation frameworks. Our approach is based on suitably chosen sets of paths in the attack graph and binary relations induced by them. We establish a general path-based criterion for semantic agreement and instantiate it with several classes of paths, obtaining sufficient conditions expressed in terms of well-foundedness of the corresponding relations.
  The resulting conditions strictly generalize two principal structural criteria for preferred and stable agreement in the literature: Dung's limited controversiality condition and the SCC-symmetry condition. In particular, Dung's result is recovered as a special case by taking the relevant path set to be the set of even paths. We further introduce two infinite sequences of path sets, each yielding a family of agreement conditions. For the first sequence, a Collapse Theorem reduces the infinitely many associated well-foundedness conditions to three essentially distinct ones, whereas the second sequence gives rise to genuinely distinct conditions with no analogous collapse. Our results apply to both finite and infinite argumentation frameworks, admit simplified formulations in the finite case, and also yield sufficient conditions for the existence of stable extensions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When an LLM agent's move fails, the harness writes the move and the failure message into the transcript and asks again. In tool calling, this record makes small models more likely to repeat the failed action. We test whether the same holds in games (Sokoban, Connect Four, Othello), where a move can fail by being illegal or by being legal but losing, both verified exactly. On fixed probe items built by perturbing a correct move, the record backfires: pooled over 8 instruction-tuned models (135M-1.7B) it makes the failed move more likely (corrective gain G = -1.38 nats per token), through the move's surface form, for illegal moves but not for losing ones, and not for every model. An exploratory analysis shows what sets the sign: after the record, the failed move's log-probability barely depends on how likely it was before (slope 0.18). Moves the model would not have played become more likely, and greedy decoding starts to produce them; moves it was about to play become less likely, but greedy decoding still repeats 38% of them. A description of the failure that omits the move string keeps the model's preference instead. Along the OLMo 2 1B post-training ladder the inversion is present in the base model and largely removed at the DPO stage. RAGEN's default models invert in RAGEN's own Sokoban format before any RL. A "do not repeat" instruction does nothing, a clean retry is the worst harness, and a decoder ban stops exact repeats but not illegal play. All measurements ran in float32 on a free Colab GPU, which first reproduced the original paper's number.

</details>

Abstract (英文): While artificial intelligence has made rapid progress in mathematical reasoning, many open problems depend on structure that is revealed only through large-scale computational exploration and analytical synthesis. One such example is Lehmer's problem from 1933. It asks whether the Mahler measure of an integer polynomial, which measures how far its roots stray from the unit circle, can be made arbitrarily small. Previously, constructing irreducible polynomials with integer coefficients and small Mahler measure has been carried out systematically only for even degrees $d \le 180$. At higher degrees, Boyd's analytical one-parameter family frequently factors, skipping nearly 50% of target degrees and leaving no general construction for arbitrary $d$. Here, we address the high-degree regime through a human-AI discovery loop, in which we extend the conversational prompting with a fast numerical pipeline, an exact symbolic verifier, and large-scale distributed search. The AI agent prototypes numerical and symbolic solvers locally, launches parallel searches across distributed clusters and works with the human researcher to extract and verify mathematical hypotheses. Through this interactive loop, we derive a five-parameter construction, which we call the Generalized Boyd algorithm. It eliminates degree-skipping and constructs irreducible polynomials with Mahler measure strictly below Boyd's limit point $α_1 \approx 1.255434$ for every target degree. Evaluated across all even degrees up to 2000, our approach improves upon six previously published values and holds 99.6% of all best known records. With this work we release the verified dataset of polynomials with minimal Mahler measure across all studied even degrees.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models often apply complex reasoning indiscriminately, leading to overthinking on simple tasks and underthinking on harder ones. We present Route-and-Generate (Rouge), a hierarchical training framework that enables LLMs to adaptively decide when and how much reasoning is required for each input. Our approach decouples reasoning effort allocation from answer generation by introducing a routing policy that allocates a fixed sampling budget across multiple reasoning formats, while the base model is optimized with GRPO using per-sample rewards. To promote efficient reasoning, we employ a rank-based reward that favors shorter correct solutions, and update the routing policy via a closed-form KL-regularized policy improvement step distilled into a neural router. This design encourages concise answers on easy problems while reserving longer reasoning for harder ones, achieving improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs across multiple reasoning benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Neuro-symbolic (NeSy) predictors that supervise only the task label suffer from reasoning shortcuts: distinct concept mappings achieve the same maximum likelihood, leaving the recovered concepts unidentifiable and unsafe for downstream reuse. We attack this failure by injecting a soft ontology prior over the latent concepts into the training objective and compiling it together with the task constraint into a smooth, decomposable probabilistic circuit. Under independent Bernoulli perception, the normalisers of the resulting structured concept posterior reduce to weighted model counts that are linear in the compiled circuit size, because the neural leaf weights fold into the circuit before evaluation. The soft compilation strategy places expert axioms in the circuit's weights rather than its support (task-structural axioms guaranteed by the data-generating process stay hard), so noisy expert axioms only redistribute prior mass and cannot empty the support. We characterise how the prior reweights label-restricted shortcut mass through differences in ontology violation energy, show that soft priors incur bounded log-density perturbations under bounded axiom noise, and prove that the hard-support counterpart can be made degenerate by a single corrupted axiom. We validate these predictions on an audited six-concept view of the real-world BDD-OIA driving benchmark and on controlled NeSy tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) based multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their adaptability to evolving task requirements. Although recent approaches enable dynamic orchestration, trajectory-level rewards they apply assign one outcome to each decision, ofen struggling to identify the contributions of individual structural changes. We propose RoleFlow, a self-evolving multi-role agentic system that refines collaboration structures through feedback-driven atomic edits and on-demand role synthesis. To make structural adaptation learnable, we introduce Single-Edit Counterfactual Policy Optimization (SECPO), which evaluates each edit against its no-edit counterpart at the same chain state and assigns targeted credit to edit-specific tokens. Across seven benchmarks spanning four domains, RoleFlow achieves the optimal average performance, surpassing the strongest competitor by 4.7 points. It leads on all three multi-hop QA benchmarks by 3.2-7.5 F1 points and also shows supremacy on mathematical reasoning, web interaction, and code generation. Further analyses demonstrate cross-benchmark transferability, training-data efficiency, and cross-executor generalization.

Abstract (英文): Sixth-generation (6G) communication systems are expected to support immersive, intent-driven, and latency-critical services through tight integration of multimodal intelligence, edge computing, and autonomous network control. In this setting, agentic AI systems can reason over heterogeneous inputs, use tools and network functions, coordinate across distributed entities, and adapt to dynamic environments. These capabilities make agentic multimodal intelligence promising for 6G applications such as semantic communications, digital twins, and zero-touch network management, but they also introduce security and privacy risks that extend beyond conventional AI and network security models. Vulnerabilities can propagate across multimodal data pipelines, memory, tools, communication interfaces, and inter-agent coordination loops. We survey multimodal intelligence and agentic AI for 6G cross-layered communication systems from the perspectives of privacy and security. We present a 6G-oriented system view spanning perception, memory, tool use, and multi-agent orchestration; analyze threat models and attack surfaces across sensing, cognition, action, memory, and communication layers; organize existing attacks into a structured taxonomy; and review mitigation strategies including prompt and memory defenses, inter-agent trust mechanisms, secure orchestration, and privacy-preserving learning. We conclude by outlining open research directions for trustworthy multimodal multi-agent 6G systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-task generalization is an important yet underexplored problem in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). Many real-world applications require multi-agent systems to generalize across complex, temporally extended tasks with diverse inter-agent coordination requirements. We introduce a specification language that combines temporal-logic task specifications with operators for expressing such requirements. We propose SpecMAGIC, a hierarchical framework that leverages the automaton structure of task specifications for subtask decomposition. A centralized high-level planner identifies and allocates compatible subtask sequences across agents, while decentralized low-level policies execute individual subtasks. This enables zero-shot generalization to new tasks by composing reusable subtask policies according to new specifications, without retraining. We establish soundness and probabilistic completeness of the high-level planner. Experiments on multi-agent navigation tasks show that SpecMAGIC substantially outperforms existing approaches on unseen task specifications and maintains strong performance as the number of agents increases.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents capable of autonomous decision-making introduce significant challenges related to safety, alignment, accountability, and human oversight. Existing Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) and responsible AI approaches provide important foundations, but many remain fragmented across system design, governance, and operational deployment. This paper presents a Three-Pillar (3P) model and framework for developing safe and trustworthy AI agents through transparency, accountability, and trustworthiness. The framework defines an evolutionary path in which increasing agent autonomy is introduced through progressive validation, structured human oversight, and auditable operational controls. We further describe how the model supports agent lifecycle monitoring, decision traceability, escalation management, and human-agent collaboration within enterprise agentic environments. By integrating oversight principles with operational mechanisms, the proposed framework provides a practical foundation for deploying AI agents that remain aligned with human intent while enabling controlled progression toward higher autonomy.

Abstract (英文): Reasoning LLMs often continue reasoning after reaching a correct answer, wasting computation and sometimes replacing it with an incorrect one. Deciding when to stop is a metareasoning problem: the model must weigh what further reasoning could achieve against its cost, given what it can already answer. Direct utility training learns a stopping representation at a chosen token price; supporting new prices can require additional controller training. We introduce BELT (BELief-based Termination), which separates outcome prediction from stopping control. It learns beliefs about current and future answers from a frozen LLM's hidden states, then fits a linear stopping policy on the frozen features. The prediction targets are independent of token price, so one probe supports different accuracy-cost trade-offs through CPU policy refitting or threshold adjustment. Averaged over five out-of-distribution (OOD) math benchmarks, BELT reduces generated tokens by 41% on Qwen3-1.7B and 51% on OLMo-3-7B-Think without loss of accuracy. The same math-trained BELT transfers to coding and agentic tasks without training on code or agentic data. In all three domains, its accuracy-cost trade-offs are competitive with or better than those of end-to-end controllers. On OLMo3, its probe has 21.2x fewer parameters than one checkpoint of the strongest baseline, OS-Pruner, and needs 55x fewer training GPU-hours than OS-Pruner's five price-specific checkpoints.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Personal intelligence systems are taking on increasingly complex everyday tasks, yet repeated questions, unnecessary confirmations, and unwanted outreach can turn capable assistance into interaction burden. Reducing this burden requires agents to remember how users want to be assisted. However, existing memory benchmarks focus on factual recall and personalized answers and rarely test how agents learn and act on interaction preferences. We introduce MemPABench, an interactive environment for on-policy evaluation of memory in personal agents. MemPABench poses long-horizon tasks requiring agents to decide when to clarify, confirm, or act autonomously. Four grounded simulated users hold 14 preferences across work and personal contexts. Their feedback spans 99 sessions over four months, with five event-driven changes per user testing memory updates. We evaluate preference selection, delivered behavior, and sensitivity to context and preference change. Evaluating four memory systems across four LLM backbones, we find that current memory brings limited gains in preference selection. Retrieval-based systems (i.e. Mem0, MemOS, simple RAG) do not outperform No Memory, while File-based memory improves accuracy but applies preferences beyond relevant contexts. Our analysis shows that current memory struggles to retrieve interaction preferences and adapt to preference changes. Even with the correct preference available, overriding the agent's default interaction habits remains challenging. The environment and dataset are available at https://mempabench.com.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World models help agents assess the consequences of their actions before acting. Many real systems, however, involve multiple actors who make their own decisions, can evolve even when the agent takes no action, and are only partially observed through event logs. We formalize these systems as event worlds and ask whether large language models can predict future observations from the log and the agent's proposed actions. We build complementary benchmarks from human Diplomacy games and real Polymarket trades. Our hierarchical framework compares prediction with and without other actors' actions, then tests multi-step rollouts, predictions for actions never taken, and usefulness to agents. Fine-tuning and more data improve prediction when all actors' recorded actions are given. With only the agent's action, models struggle when other actors strongly affect the outcome, and larger models and longer histories help little. This gap also limits multi-step prediction and agent decisions, with models overestimating plan success. Yet imagined experience and initialization from world models can still improve policy learning. Anticipating other actors' actions remains a central challenge for event world modeling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents are often evaluated by reducing execution traces to labels such as final-answer correctness, tool verification, and fault propagation. These labels are measurements produced by parsers, behavioral detectors, and harness rules, and they may not fully capture what the trace shows. We study this in a tool-use setting built on GSM8K, where agents call a calculator whose outputs are occasionally corrupted, giving clear ground truth for what the tool returned, what the model later used, and what the detectors reported. We audit three trace-level detectors across six production models and 30 problems per model. Final correctness, visible re-verification, and fault propagation can disagree in systematic ways. A key failure mode is silent tool-output override: the calculator returns a corrupted value, but the model later uses a different, often correct, value without calling the tool again, so the bad value stops propagating while a verification detector records no re-check. Human review of 90 traces agrees perfectly with the automated labels for final correctness (67/67), fault impact (19/19), and fault relevance (18/18), but only 67/81 for verification; all 14 disagreements fall into four recurring patterns. Execution limits also change the reported result: in an ablation across two models, one ranges from 21/30 to 27/30 correct as step and token budgets change, while the other stays between 27/30 and 29/30. These findings motivate measurement transparency: an automated label should come with the trace evidence behind it, whether the question the detector asks applied to that trace, whether the run was cut off by an execution limit, and any human corrections. Our trace viewer makes this information inspectable alongside the reported results.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents now run experiments and report findings, so research produces claims faster than researchers can check them. Each empirical claim implies a property that must hold over its cases, but it states that property only in prose. Without an explicit property, no test can decide which evidence would falsify a claim, so validation cannot keep pace with the claims agents produce. To make claims testable, we approximate a claim's semantics by abstracting its property skeleton, a parameterized relation that recurs across claims. We present ARATest, a property-based testing library that checks these skeletons as executable tests. A typed declaration binds a claim to a skeleton and fixes the claim's compared quantities, cases, scope, and numerical policy. A shared case type makes every generator supply cases in the claim's statistical unit. A three-valued semantics assigns each property one of three verdicts: holds, violated, or inconclusive. Every violation carries a replayable witness. A default generator reads the evidence that agent-native research artifacts (ARA) link to each claim, and auto-research generators dispatch an agent to run released code for fresh cases. Our DissClaimer plugin supplies that agent and runs these generators as continuous testing.
  Of 243 claims in 33 ARA artifacts, 54 received faithful declarations bound to evidence: 32 held, eight were violated, and 14 remained inconclusive. Two violations exposed errors in published papers that their authors confirmed. ARATest detected all 553 seeded faults without a false alarm on 19 valid controls, outperforming two LLM judges. Fresh runs exposed behaviors that reported evidence could not show. These results suggest that one fixed test can check explicit claim properties against both reported and fresh evidence.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A generally intelligent system acquires new skills efficiently without requiring humans to redesign its software scaffold for every new task. Yet language models rarely act alone. A harness is a symbolic program that orchestrates calls to the model, handling perception, memory, tool use, recovery, and inference allocation on the model's behalf. ARC-AGI-3 is an interactive benchmark in which agents must explore unfamiliar environments, figure out their goals on the fly, and keep learning as they go. On it, human-steered systems already report ceiling scores across the public games, so we know effective designs exist. We ask whether frontier models can build and improve such a harness on their own. Through a fixed coding bootstrap, each model constructs a complete task harness, acts through that harness on five development games, and revises the entire codebase twice. Every version is evaluated on twenty games that never influence adaptation. We measure task performance with Relative Human Action Efficiency (RHAE), which combines level completion with action efficiency relative to human baselines, and record dollar-denominated inference cost as a practical resource measure. Across 27 lineages, harness performance ranges from zero to near-ceiling scores. Two revisions raise mean held-out RHAE by only 2.80 points (95% interval [-1.44,7.35]). Development gains overstate held-out improvement, and reductions in inference cost can come at the expense of task performance. Human-steered systems demonstrate further headroom. These findings reveal a gap between autonomous harness construction and reliable adaptation under this two-revision protocol: even strong task performance does not ensure that subsequent revisions generalize or preserve existing capabilities.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Frontier language models now score above 90% on popular knowledge benchmarks and are increasingly deployed as agents that carry out multi-hour professional work. Yet no existing benchmark tests whether they can perform the real-world, economically valuable work of a professional physical engineer, such as modelling physics and legal code requirements across a multi-format project corpus, optimising material and installation costs, and issuing 2D/3D engineering design packages that a licensed reviewer would sign off on. Existing engineering benchmarks focus on textbook question answering, isolated tool calls or self-contained toy design problems. As a result, most of these benchmarks are already saturated by leading agents and solving them is far away from delivering real engineering design. We introduce PLE, a benchmark of 85 long-horizon, agentic tasks in professional physical engineering consisting of electrical, civil, structural and mechanical engineering, where solving a task means completing a realistic, billable engineering deliverable. PLE combines two complementary curation paths. Expert-Authored tasks are created end to end by practising engineers from a professional data provider's expert pool. Co-Authored tasks start from problem statements written by practising engineers; an adversarial Creator-Solver agent pipeline constructs each task's realistic project environment, executable grader and reference deliverables before sending them to expert engineers for validation and difficulty intensification. We evaluate a variety of frontier LLMs under seven publicly available agent harnesses. Even the strongest configuration reaches a mean score of only 50.6%, while spending 27M tokens and 63 minutes per task on average, demonstrating the substantial gap between current agents and autonomous physical engineering design.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents that act on live Web data must combine heterogeneous evidence, remember what they have done and improve from outcomes without weakening their own safeguards. We study this problem in autonomous investing, where every action is irreversible and based on untrusted Web content. We present Steward, a multi-agent LLM system that draws the boundary of self-evolution in its type system. First, nine role-specialised agents decide jointly: four analysts read news feeds, SEC filings, macroeconomic series and market data and emit typed reports, from which a Portfolio Manager sets target positions that a protected Risk Manager audits. Second, a seven-layer shared memory, assembled by code from the system's own record, gives each decision its position context, the market narrative, calibration and the agent's past decisions. Third, the system improves itself by review: code grades every trade and forecast nightly and writes the grades into memory, and each quarter a reflector proposes prompt learnings that a deterministic gate applies reversibly and replay of recorded inputs verifies. All three operate around an invariant core of order arithmetic, risk rules and discipline-agent prompts that no self-edit can reach. In a five-month live deployment (102 trading days, 1,632 LLM calls), the system contained a 24.6% sector drawdown to a 0.3% loss; in replay of its recorded inputs, a single model given the same raw data traded more and lost 1.35 points against the market over five days where the joint design showed no significant loss, and no injected instruction was followed; and a 2015-2026 backtest shows the core alone lowering the maximum drawdown under all 50 random entry policies. The reflector traced the dominant failure to its root cause, and the gate applied a learning that the agent adopted on trigger evenings and on no control evening. Code: https://github.com/yebof/quant-agent

</details>

Abstract (英文): Unlike conventional GUI agents, personalized GUI agents require user preferences to guide sequential execution over changing GUI states. This raises two challenges: determining HOW each preference should alter execution strategy and WHEN it should take effect along the interaction trajectory. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate overall task success, offering limited insight into these questions. We introduce PrefDiag-Bench with two components: (1) a categorization of preferences into five types, characterizing HOW they affect execution; and (2) a stage-wise diagnostic framework with 1,664 probes, tracing WHEN preferences should take effect and where failures occur. We evaluate eleven GUI agent systems, including end-to-end agents and agentic workflows, under two forms of user context: explicit user profiles and behavioral memory logs. Although existing agents achieve relatively high completion rates on non-personalized tasks, performance is substantially lower on preference-personalized tasks. Even the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves only 47.17% on Process preferences under explicit user profiles. Agents struggle particularly with Ranking and Process preferences. Further analysis reveals two key capability bottlenecks: selecting relevant evidence from user history and recognizing when preferences should take effect during execution. Controlled interventions that explicitly provide relevant evidence improve end-to-end task success by up to 7.06%. PrefDiag-Bench offers a more systematic evaluation of GUI agents' personalization capabilities beyond overall task success. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Zhixin-L/PrefDiag-Bench.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI control protocols aim to get useful work from models that may be misaligned by monitoring their behaviour. One approach uses a second copy of the same model as the monitor. This avoids relying on a weaker reviewer the model could outsmart, but risks collusion: the model, now monitoring itself, may cover up its own misbehaviour. One remedy is to fine-tune the monitor on whatever examples of misbehaviour the control team has caught. We ask whether this training also removes collusion that the control team has not yet found. We fine-tune Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B to install five policy-monitor behaviour pairs, correct the monitor on 300 honest reviews of one caught collusion, and measure collusion on the other four in unseen prompt formats. Averaged over the three seeds of each family, correction reduces held-out collusion from 91% to 14-20% under three of the four methods we compare (individual seeds 10-32%), while a control fine-tuned on the same number of unrelated examples leaves 56-93%. Family-average accuracy on control items changes by at most five points and capability is preserved, apart from a drop in mathematical accuracy on one Llama seed. Under the tested training configuration, methods with similar caught-task accuracy differ in held-out transfer, from 58% to 83% average reduction, so the caught behaviour alone does not reveal how far a correction reaches. Catching one collusion lets the control team reduce, but not remove, collusion it has not caught, by retraining the monitor alone.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scaling large language models has substantially improved linguistic fluency, yet reliable autonomous agents require more than fluent generation. In multi-turn inference, current agentic systems often produce semantically plausible but structurally ungrounded reasoning, especially under incomplete observations. We propose Structure-Grounded Uncertainty Reduction (SGUR), a training-free framework that complements token-level generation with active uncertainty reduction over structured hypothesis spaces. SGUR represents incomplete observations as a query graph and aligns them with candidate hypothesis graphs through a prior-weighted partial graph matching mechanism, where database-derived empirical priors assign greater importance to decision-relevant relations rather than treating all graph edges uniformly. The resulting inference state is iteratively refined by an active information-gain strategy that selects the next most informative node to query, systematically pruning the candidate space. We instantiate SGUR on a challenging PrimeKG-derived sequential medical diagnosis benchmark, demonstrating that structural grounding can partially substitute for model scale: starting from only two observed symptoms, SGUR achieves 92.5% Hit@3, outperforming the strongest Graph-RAG baseline by 9.4 percentage points while using approximately 5x fewer tokens. Notably, SGUR enables smaller backbone models to match or exceed the performance of models up to 10x larger, suggesting that structural grounding provides a more robust and efficient paradigm for scaling agent performance than relying exclusively on increasing model size.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Medical knowledge evolves as clinical guidelines revise recommendations, yet whether large language models (LLMs) faithfully incorporate such changes remains unclear. We introduce ChronoEvo, comprising 1,709 recommendation-level revisions across 12 forms of medical knowledge change, to distinguish recognition of revised guidance from incorporation into clinical recommendations. In the primary model, 90.8% of responses recognized revisions and 80.2% were clinically correct, but only 58.5% fully incorporated guidance, yielding a 32.3-percentage-point recognition-incorporation gap. This dissociation persisted across 14 models and was corroborated by two clinicians' adjudicated assessment of 300 responses, yielding a 40.3-percentage-point gap in the validation sample. Changes in recommendation strength and patient eligibility were associated with retention of outdated guidance. A revision-salience intervention reduced outdated-content retention and improved clinical correctness but did not significantly improve full incorporation. These findings identify faithful incorporation of evolving medical knowledge as a distinct challenge for clinical LLM reliability, even when updated guideline evidence is available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The claim that speculative-decoding acceptance rate decays as a chain-of-thought grows longer has become a de facto industry narrative, motivating draft-model co-design and position-aware training. Nearly all direct evidence comes from trained drafters whose offline training length is far shorter than inference, so a purely positional law has never been isolated. We present the first systematic, full-length measurement of acceptance rate as a function of generation position on MATH-500 (all 500 problems), under greedy and sampling decoding, with two independent drafts against DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B: a same-family draft (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B) and a cross-family draft (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct), yielding about 1.78M verification rounds. Using within-problem paired tests, we find that after isolating a greedy-decoding repetition pathology, acceptance rate does not decay with position in any of the four configurations within the supported ranges (greedy 0-8K, sampling 0-12K); the second half of each trajectory is slightly but significantly higher than the first. A controlled temperature sweep shows that the apparent sampling-mode degradation of high-entropy reflection tokens is a visibility artifact of argmax agreement, not a drop in expected acceptance probability. Rejections concentrate with high precision on reflection trigger tokens such as "wait", "however" and "check", an entropy-selective failure that a lightweight three-arm LoRA trajectory-alignment intervention (uniform, reflection-marked, entropy-weighted) fails to improve in mean accepted length. We thus supply the positional baseline that independent draft-target pairs were missing, and localize the residual failure to decision-point entropy rather than position.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Social world models predict how interactions develop after an action, helping agents negotiate, cooperate and mediate. In these situations, one action can lead to several plausible futures, hence predicting only one future can overlook consequential alternatives, while exploring them through repeated simulations increases inference cost. We propose DSWM, a Distributional Social World Model that predicts alternative responses, their probabilities and immediate and final outcome values in one output per candidate action. We train a 4B model on repeated simulations using supervised fine-tuning and GRPO. At inference time, the actor uses these possible consequences to choose an action. Across three seen and two unseen environments, with interaction backbones absent from training collection, DSWM achieves the best average performance on 13 of 15 metrics. Ablations show that retaining multiple futures and their predicted probabilities improves outcomes in all five environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Open-world geographic artificial intelligence (GeoAI) agents can transform public geospatial data into accessible decision support for real-world challenges, but must balance autonomous reasoning with reliable spatial analysis and human oversight. We present a topology-guided GeoAI agentic framework for urban flood screening that takes a place name, drawn region, or uploaded boundary and returns ranked candidate basins with an interactive map and report. A deterministic controller retrieves public elevation, rainfall, soil, land-cover, exposure, and infrastructure layers, extracts terrain depressions with discrete Morse theory, and ranks basins with a relative flood-priority score. The language model acts only at recorded decision points: selecting a persistence threshold from a precomputed candidate table, drafting recommendations from computed evidence, and reviewing each recommendation against the same evidence. Three human-in-the-loop checkpoints let users revise the threshold, factor weights, or recommendation. In a College Park, Maryland case study, the selected threshold reduced 278 raw terrain minima to 70 basins, with 77% of these basins' lowest points also identified as sinks by a separately computed eight-direction (D8) flow-routing model. Across four further study areas, agreement ranged from 61% to 83%. By combining open data, explicit spatial reasoning, traceable evidence, and human oversight, the framework enables transparent first-pass flood screening while keeping consequential recommendations inspectable and user-controlled.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Autoregressive encoder-decoder speech recognition models trained with teacher forcing can suffer from exposure bias, limiting their ability to recover from their own prediction errors at inference time. Recent work has shown that reinforcement learning with Group Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) improves speech recognition in major languages with LLM-based models. We extend this line of work to smaller, non-LLM encoder-decoder ASR models, using Whisper-large-v3 as a case study across 51 African languages. The benefits of this approach are most pronounced when training data is scarce or when input audio is noisy or degraded. Our resulting model, SunflowerASR, achieves the best published macro-averaged WER on the AfriVox-v2 (0.227) and SimbaBench (0.372) benchmarks, and is publicly available.

Abstract (英文): Safety probes trained on a model's activations are a standard tool for monitoring language models in real time. Each time a new model is released or a new harm area needs to be monitored, the probe must be retrained, which requires new labels and a choice of probe architecture. Yet neither the model nor the labels tell us in advance how well the resulting probe will perform. Finding out requires training and comparing many probe configurations. In this paper, we ask whether probe performance can instead be predicted in advance from model size and the number of labels. Existing safety datasets are insufficient for this, as they are either limited in scale or already saturated. We therefore construct six new safety datasets of sufficient size and difficulty. On these datasets, we train two probe architectures on 20 base models from five model families, with 32 to $10^{6}$ labels for each model. We measure performance using probe error, defined as 1 - AUROC, where lower values are better. We find that probe error follows two simple scaling laws. First, the lowest probe error a model can reach falls as a power law in its size. Second, probe error decreases towards its floor as the number of examples increases; the slope increases as the target model size increases. Both laws hold across tasks, model families and probe architectures, and only their fitted constants differ. When fitted on the smallest models of a family, the laws predict the error of the next larger model to within 0.02 in 72% of cases. They also identify whether more labels or a larger model is the cheaper way to reach a target error in 73% of cases. Finally, they choose the probe architecture with an extra error of only 0.006 on average when they pick the worse one. These laws allow safety researchers to determine how to create the best probe for any given model and label budget.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When a multilingual LLM agrees with a user's false premise, is it being sycophantic, or does it simply not know better in that language? Current research does not distinguish these cases: they record only whether the model agreed, not why. We introduce the SC-matrix, that crosses a model's competence (whether it answers correctly under no pressure) with its sycophancy (whether it maintains that answer under pressure). Our results show that (1) identical aggregate sycophancy rates can mask different failure compositions, (2) high competence in a language does not always protect against sycophancy, and (3) reasoning-oriented mitigations outperform directive-based strategies. We find that multilingual sycophancy is not a single phenomenon but a composite of different failures that require distinct diagnosis.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An LLM agent can retrieve a memory correctly yet use it incorrectly after its validity state changes. We study governance-state corruption: faults that alter which retained records enter model-visible context while stored content and tasks remain fixed. Across six providers, 731 benchmark-grounded bases yield 2,080 matched fault pairs. In offline control-plane checks, corruption changes the operative projection in 96.25% of pairs, while oracle-backed enforcement restores it in 100%. For downstream evaluation, a deterministically selected, non-probability LongMemEval/Memora panel resolves 298 of 300 matched triplets (238 distinct bases). Clean-minus-corrupted contrasts are 40.54 percentage points in LongMemEval binary correctness (95% base-cluster interval [30.32, 50.71]) and 30.60 points in rescaled Memora FAMA ([23.31, 37.69]); both sources also show recovery. OAKS Novel native option parity holds for all 6,714 resolved outputs. Full-cohort frozen-scorer corruption/recovery effects (-27.55/+27.51 points) remain descriptive proxies, not validated native semantic population effects. These bounded findings motivate evaluating memory control-state integrity alongside retrieval accuracy.

Abstract (英文): Large language models have been reported to encode the harmfulness of a request separately from their decision to refuse it, and latent safety monitors such as Latent Guard build on this report by classifying prompts as harmful from the internal activations of the model, with the aim of catching harmful intent even when the model complies. Whether these representations give a signal of harm that survives distribution shift and is independent of model behaviour is unclear. We test this on out-of-distribution prompts made by rewriting existing harmful and benign requests with surface rewording, persuasive framing and jailbreak-style wrappers. Across 15 instruction-tuned LLMs and 38 activation-based readers, we compare harm detection with that of TF-IDF and sentence-embedding classifiers, with independently trained safety classifiers, and with the refusal of each model. Activations are read at the last token of the user instruction, where the model has read the whole request, and at the last chat-template token before the reply, where the planned response can be represented. Because models disagree about which prompts to refuse, we can separate a signal of the harmfulness of the prompt from a signal of the refusal decision of the model. At the end of the instruction, the representation is largely independent of the refusal behaviour of each model but degrades under distribution shift, so that a TF-IDF classifier ranks harm better than the original Latent Guard in every model we test, and linear and MLP probes do about as well as sentence-embedding classifiers trained on the same examples. The representation just before generation detects more, catching 44 percent of harmful prompts at a matched false-alarm rate against 32 percent for the refusal of the model, but that gain follows the refusal decision itself, and part of it comes from refusals that string-matching evaluation misses.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When tool-use agents write files, execute code, or send data, their final responses do not explain the resulting security events, and heterogeneous runtimes expose the relevant evidence through incompatible interfaces. We present AgentTriage, an executable contract for replayable security diagnosis across heterogeneous runtimes. The contract admits conclusions whose claims cite canonical runtime events and requires abstention when mandatory evidence is missing. Runtime overlays normalize native observations, while a deterministic pipeline joins invocation and result lifecycles, constructs a typed provenance graph, selects a policy-decisive slice, and binds claims to recorded evidence. Given the same normalized trace, overlay version, and pre-run policy oracle, the diagnosis can be recomputed and its claims re-checked without language-model judgment. Across five runtimes, one contract captures all 159 applicable executions, with an audit of native and synthesized identifiers. On 120 adjudicated cases, AgentTriage achieves 0.858 grounded joint diagnosis, 0.966 required-evidence coverage, and a 0.036 unsupported-claim rate, improving joint diagnosis by 0.175 (95% CI [0.092, 0.258]) over the strongest same-schema baseline. These results quantify the precision of replayable diagnosis and the observation boundaries that require abstention. We release an open-source implementation and selected sanitized evidence at https://github.com/AsnGs/agenttriage-open-source.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM memory is widely used in long-term conversation understanding. Agentic memory systems often use mutation, structural organization, learned retrieval strategies, or other complex operations. Is this complexity always necessary? We study Raw-RAG, a naive RAG baseline using dense retrieval and reranking over raw history to supply evidence to a reader LLM. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval, MEME, and MemoryAgentBench, Raw-RAG ranks first among the compared memory systems in six of seven benchmark-model settings, outperforming the strongest compared agentic memory system by 0.13-5.53 points. We investigate why raw-history retrieval remains competitive. Evidence analysis shows that its higher coverage of necessary evidence can offset lower utilization. Useful evidence includes both answer values and the relations and dependency paths needed to derive them. Even complete access to transformed memory stores leaves an accuracy gap relative to raw evidence, highlighting the importance of preserving raw information. These findings challenge the necessity of complex agentic memory for the evaluated conversation tasks and establish naive RAG as an essential baseline.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI-assisted editing can produce loan documents that preserve familiar issuer layouts while changing the applicant's identity. We investigated whether experienced underwriters and loan processors identify such forgeries in first-pass, document-only review. Three practitioners recruited through Upwork each received one applicant package drawn from 134 forged PDFs derived from 47 genuine financial documents supplied with full consent. The edits preserved amounts, dates and balances; identifier inconsistencies introduced by the pipeline were knowingly retained before distribution. Reviewers were not told that files had been altered and returned 132 file-level judgements. Checking their explanations against the originals yielded 14 edit-supported concerns (10.6%): 12 Suspicious judgements and two Insufficient information judgements. All supported concerns involved identifier disagreements within or across documents, and no reported visual cue identified an actual edit. Thus, approximately one in ten judgements contained evidence of the forgery, despite known inconsistencies remaining in the files. These observations motivate evaluating consistency checks and independent source verification alongside human review. With three reviewer-package pairs and no genuine-file controls, the findings describe this exploratory setting rather than population-wide detection accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This paper presents AgentDP, which aims to deploy pre-trained AI models on heterogeneous embedded devices by fully automating model optimization and adaptation (O&A) with Large Language Model (LLM) agents. AgentDP not only significantly reduces human effort via automation but also enhances model inference efficacy by integrating LLMs with device-specific domain knowledge. To deliver a robust automation framework, AgentDP develops novel solutions to address three key technical challenges: 1) To help users elicit clear requirements, we propose a user requirement standardization module; 2) To mitigate the execution environment mismatch between AI models and heterogeneous embedded devices, we design an operator-aware model partition paradigm that enables different parts of the model to be efficiently executed on distinct inference engines. 3) To avoid re-searching optimization hyperparameters for new tasks, we propose a configuration reuse module that heuristically retrieves previous hyperparameter configurations for efficient reuse. Extensive experiments demonstrate that by fully automating the entire O&A process, AgentDP reduces entire model adaptation time by up to 80% compared with manual O&A while achieving higher task accuracy (more than 13% on average) than the baselines. Additionally, a comprehensive user study also exhibits the robust generalizability of AgentDP in adapting to different user requirements for heterogeneous embedded devices.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study generative reward models (GenRMs) as online preference providers for policy optimization in creative writing. Across Qwen3 reward-model sizes from 0.6B to 14B, Thinking judges trained with GRPO have higher static Gold-label accuracy than Answer-Only judges trained with SFT. This ranking often reverses when the judges train policies through online DPO: Answer-Only rewarders yield higher peak and top-five-average policy Elo in seven of eight matched settings under a fixed, learned 32B Gold evaluator. Intermediate Thinking-judge checkpoints can also outperform later, more statically accurate checkpoints as rewarders. Sweeps over rewarder size, policy size, training duration and DPO regularization show substantial benefits from reward-model capacity, including judges larger than the policy. We analyze trajectories with evaluator-specific Elo arenas, conditional match-resampling intervals and descriptive scaling fits. These results demonstrate an evaluator-rewarder gap in the studied pipelines: fixed-distribution judging accuracy does not determine downstream optimization quality. The comparison changes judge-training objective as well as response format, and the main ranking is conditional on its learned reference rather than a universal human-quality result. A limited follow-up using a different learned judge is inconclusive overall.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) increasingly operate in multi-agent systems where they collaborate by responding to others' choices, creating endogenous feedback loops with vulnerability to social influence, herding behavior and runaway cascades. Following the design of the classic Music Lab experiment, we tested this vulnerability in large populations of LLM agents that recommended unfamiliar songs, with or without exposure to cumulative recommendation counts. Exposure led to runaway cascades that generated extreme and unpredictable collective recommendations increasingly divergent from a song's intrinsic appeal. Compared to humans, LLM populations were generally far more susceptible to herding behavior, and unlike humans, LLM cascades were less able to self-correct. This vulnerability has implications for multi-agent collaboration in recommender systems, automated evaluation, complex problem-solving, and unauthorized conspiracies.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models reduce computation through sparse activation but still incur substantial storage costs. Mixed-precision post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces these costs by quantizing different model components at different bit widths, but existing methods often struggle to preserve generation quality under tight bit budgets. We identify two key challenges: (1) nonlinear quantization errors at extremely low precision are underestimated when cross-layer propagation is ignored; (2) direct router fine-tuning may cause routing drift, and calibration gains may not generalize to downstream tasks. To address these issues, we propose ProQuant, a weight-only PTQ framework for MoE models. Prune-or-Prioritize Mixed-Precision Allocation (PoP) jointly optimizes expert pruning and bit-width assignment. It allows higher-precision experts to substitute for severely degraded ones while improving bit-budget utilization. Routing-Consistent RMSNorm Finetune (RoCo) adapts expert input representations through mathematically equivalent transformations. It compensates for quantization errors to reduce routing drift. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ProQuant outperforms existing methods across diverse generative tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/ThrowBug/ProQuant.git.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World models have recently emerged as a promising direction toward developing generalist agents capable of understanding, predicting, and interacting with diverse environments. However, despite growing interest, the concept of a world model remains loosely defined, encompassing approaches ranging from predictive dynamics models and learned simulators to models that jointly generate future observations and actions. This introductory paper aims to clarify the concept of world models and provide a unified perspective on their role in developing generalist agents. Starting from the partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) framework, we connect world modeling to classical control, dynamical systems, signal processing, and reinforcement learning. We introduce different approaches to world modeling, emphasizing the knowledge they capture, their learning objectives, and how they support decision-making. We further explore the relationship between world modeling and policy learning, highlighting how these capabilities can be unified through conditional prediction without requiring separate models. Rather than offering an exhaustive survey of existing methods, this paper focuses on fundamental concepts, modeling principles, and connections, with the goal of providing an accessible and unified introduction to world models for generalist agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents can evolve by learning from accumulated experience. Existing approaches extract reusable external knowledge, such as skills and memories, from experience to guide subsequent task solving, with some further internalizing this knowledge through model training. However, different ways of analyzing and transforming the same experience can yield knowledge with substantially different utility, even when the resulting guidance appears reasonable. Inspired by consistency regularization, we identify consistent utility gains across different ways of analyzing the same experience, rather than relying on any single way of analyzing it. We introduce SPLICE, a framework for consistency-regularized knowledge-model co-evolution. It constructs evolution views by perturbing how the same experience is analyzed, producing different knowledge candidates. Knowledge updates are then guided by common gain, which accounts for utility gains and their consistency across views. During model evolution, self-distillation internalizes the evolved knowledge alongside reinforcement learning, using knowledge associated with consistent utility degradation as a contrastive reference. The two processes interleave, with model updates driving further knowledge evolution. Experiments across diverse domains show that SPLICE outperforms knowledge evolution and model training baselines by up to 7.1 and 4.6 points in average accuracy, while its evolved knowledge reduces output tokens per correct solution by up to 44% and transfers across model sizes and families.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Replacing the judge can move a benchmark score, and models can be trained to hide capabilities. Both put the evaluator in question: what can it certify? We develop a partition theory of evaluation from one principle: judgement is bounded by perception. An agent's discrimination is the partition of its inputs into classes it cannot tell apart. Discrimination is necessary for capability but not sufficient, so bounds on it bear directly on whether evaluation can show that a model lacks an ability. We prove a one-sided bound. An examinee whose partition refines its evaluator's has the discrimination to pass every test the evaluator can grade, provided each input has an acceptable reply. We define the echo test, which accepts any reply the evaluator perceives as identical to the input, and show that it is complete for the tests the evaluator can grade. Passing therefore certifies at most a floor on discrimination, never a ceiling, and only a sharper evaluator can raise it. Used to detect concealment, echo-test failure points the wrong way: under exhaustive probing it flags every honestly weaker examinee and no concealer that presents as the evaluator's peer. Exact imitation of a weaker model is undetectable by any behavioural evaluator. We extend the bound to ensembles of monitors and to channels a model controls, explain published findings on judge disagreement and sandbagging, and derive testable predictions. A safety case that argues from behavioural evaluation alone that a model lacks an ability must assume the model's cooperation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The increasing adoption of generative AI presents new opportunities while raising AI governance and regulatory challenges, particularly for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) navigating the European Union Artificial Intelligence Act (EU AI Act). To support AI governance education, this study developed TESSA, a governance-oriented educational artifact within the OpenAI Custom GPT environment. Following a design science research approach, TESSA was evaluated against baseline ChatGPT using 100 benchmark scenarios through governance alignment assessment, human expert review, and consistency testing. Preliminary LLM-assisted assessment showed governance alignment rates of 90% for TESSA and 83% for baseline ChatGPT, while independent validation by an AI regulation law practitioner yielded 71% and 70%, respectively. Human expert review indicated that both systems provided generally strong governance guidance, while TESSA maintained high consistency across repeated executions. The findings suggest that governance-oriented grounding can support structured, practical, and stable AI-assisted compliance guidance for SMEs. The study contributes (i) a governance-oriented Custom GPT artifact for AI governance education, (ii) a human-validated regulatory update mechanism, and (iii) a multidimensional evaluation framework for governance-oriented AI artifact.

Abstract (英文): Bayesian networks for continuous variables often rely on restrictive assumptions: linear Gaussian models lack flexibility to capture nonlinear dependencies, while non-parametric approaches provide greater flexibility at a high computational cost. This creates a gap for modeling scenarios where variables are approximately Gaussian but exhibit nonlinear relationships. We introduce Semi-Linear Gaussian Bayesian Networks (SLGBN), a novel class of continuous Bayesian networks designed to bridge this gap, preserving Gaussian conditional distributions while introducing nonlinear conditional mean functions. The proposed framework provides a unified probabilistic framework combining linear Gaussian and nonlinear Gaussian conditional models within the same Bayesian network Nonlinear conditional mean functions are estimated using generalized additive models (GAMs) with penalized B-splines. We present parameter estimation procedures for both types of nodes, as well as a greedy hill-climbing algorithm for learning the network structure under the SLGBN framework. To assess its performance, we compare SLGBN with other continuous Bayesian network models, including semiparametric Bayesian networks (SPBN) and Linear Gaussian Bayesian networks (LGBN). Experimental results evaluate both goodness of fit and the ability to recover the underlying network structure. The results show that, in the presence of nonlinear Gaussian relationships, SLGBN achieves better fit and improved structure recovery compared to alternative models, particularly when combined with the Extended Bayesian Information Criterion (EBIC*) score. Furthermore, by retaining Gaussian conditional distributions, SLGBN offers computational advantages over fully semiparametric approaches.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scientific discovery often requires identifying a small number of high-performing designs in vast, constrained search spaces. This is difficult when evaluations are costly, feasible solutions are sparse, parameters are strongly coupled, and physical constraints are strict. Recent AI for AI methods can automatically improve algorithms and optimization procedures. However, general purpose approaches are not designed around scientific priors, physical constraints, and limited evaluation budgets. We introduce ADAPT for Science, a multi-agent framework that treats scientific optimization as two coupled decisions: where to search and how to search. ADAPT uses historical designs to identify promising and diverse initial conditions, progressively concentrates computation on favorable search directions, and evolves the optimization procedure from execution feedback. We evaluate ADAPT on the design of gravitational wave detectors using a differentiable physics simulator and eight unseen detector topologies. Under matched scientific evaluation budgets, ADAPT achieves a mean loss of 0.204 +/- 0.101, reducing loss by 54.9% relative to the best classical optimizer and by 62.2% relative to the best general purpose agentic evolution baseline. ADAPT achieves the lowest loss on all unseen topologies among the evaluated baselines. ADAPT provides a step toward scientific optimization systems that continuously learn from prior experience and their own search outcomes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Active inference casts perception, learning and planning as variational inference, where the Expected Free Energy (EFE) defines the planning objective. Recent work showed that EFE minimization can be written as Variational Free Energy (VFE) minimization on a generative model augmented with epistemic priors, and that the resulting entropy-corrected objective is minimized by sum-product message passing (SP-MP) on modified factor functions. These formulations treat model uncertainty as inference over a finite set of known models. We extend them to planning during learning, where beliefs over the observation and transition parameters $θ_A$, $θ_B$ are Dirichlet priors. Closed-form Dirichlet updates are usually obtained by a structured mean-field factorization between parameters and states, but under exactly that factorization the information-gain corrections vanish. We resolve this by giving every factor its own parameter prior, frozen during a rollout. Conjugacy then makes each factor belief a product of a marginal over the categorical variables and a one-step Bayes posterior of the parameters. This reintroduces the datum-dependent parameter posteriors that mean-field factorization destroys. We show that evaluating the epistemic priors under the resulting Bethe joint recovers the information gains. Stationarity of this single closed-form objective yields SP-MP whose factor functions are augmented by the information-gain weight. Experiments on two grid POMDPs, in which only a planner that learns the model finds the safe route, show that the method plans to efficiently learn $θ_A$ and $θ_B$. It reaches the goal on every episode, whereas SP-MP planners with the same inference update plateau or take the risky route.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Population graph learning supports brain disorder diagnosis by sharing information across subjects. Its effectiveness, however, depends on whether the relationships guiding this exchange are relevant to diagnosis. Similarity in demographic or acquisition attributes does not necessarily indicate diagnostic agreement, and heterogeneous subjects may require different representation updates within the same population graph. We propose PopDRP, a framework that combines diagnosis-guided relation refinement with hierarchical selective state-space learning. Using only training labels, Contrastive Affinity Refinement calibrates attribute-based affinities according to diagnostic agreement. Neural Graph Rectification then refines these relationships using fused functional-connectivity and non-imaging representations, guided by graph reconstruction and dual-view alignment objectives. On the resulting graph, a Selective Pyramid State-Space backbone integrates input-dependent state updates, graph diffusion, and hierarchical pooling and unpooling to learn population representations at multiple resolutions. Together, these components connect the refinement of inter-subject relationships with selective representation learning over the population graph. Experiments on ABIDE and ADHD-200 under stratified ten-fold cross-validation show improved accuracy and AUC over the compared baselines. Ablation studies further support the contributions of relation refinement and the selective hierarchical backbone to classification performance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present TwinStore, a source-available synthetic consumer platform for physical retail built on vision--language models. TwinStore turns a store into a digital twin---from 82 ready-made formats, a floor-plan image or phone photos of its shelves---and fills it with synthetic shoppers who plan a trip, walk the aisles and decide what to buy by looking at rendered shelves with packs, price tags and promotion signs. Every trip ends in a receipt, so a layout, price, pack or promotion can be tried on the same shoppers before it reaches the store. Around this core, TwinStore provides 3D playback, paired comparisons and layout optimization, shopper interviews, a trade-area model, counterfactual edits of real shelf photos, a marketing studio with eleven tools, and an assistant that turns a plain sentence into a drafted study or an answer grounded in a run's own numbers. In a held-out validation on eleven public datasets, simulated receipts approach the households' own natural variation (category-mix divergence 0.017--0.018 against a real drift of 0.012), price elasticities fall inside the range measured in the field, and three independent decision models agree. TwinStore runs on cloud or on-device models and is distributed with installers for Windows, macOS and Linux.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can translate natural-language optimization problems into solver programs, but reliably producing correct formulations and implementations remains challenging. Existing approaches improve optimization modeling through agentic workflows or task-specific post-training. This raises a practical question: can a lightweight harness using a single model role achieve competitive performance without additional training? We introduce OPTICYCLE and OPTISTATE, two lightweight inference harnesses for optimization modeling, and evaluate them on nine benchmarks comprising 2,634 problems using 11 Qwen variants spanning 0.6B-27B parameters. Our results show that execution-guided revision consistently improves macro accuracy over direct generation for all evaluated models, with gains of up to 49.46 percentage points. Despite their simplicity, both harnesses outperform six adapted agentic workflows in same-model comparisons and four evaluated post-trained optimization models at comparable model sizes, without additional training. Explicit formulation state provides further but model-dependent improvements, suggesting that additional structure is not universally beneficial.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diffusion language models (DLMs) decode multiple tokens in parallel, so individually valid tokens can be incompatible together under a schema. Exact joint inference over a grammar graph enforces compatibility, but it requires token-labeled successors across many hypothetical parser configurations. Encoding stacks and field histories in compiled states can cause exponential growth, which forces prior exact decoders to fix property orders and exclude valid outputs. We present DConstrain, which compiles schemas into context-parameterized transition programs. Cold compilation specializes token consumption to local grammar states while retaining context-dependent stack operations, environment guards, and caller-dependent deferred parsing symbolically. Tracking field histories as runtime values supports arbitrary property order without enumerating field subsets offline. At runtime, warm compilation binds reached configurations to these programs to construct exact successors, overlapping with the model forward pass because graph construction requires no model scores. Furthermore, token-effect grouping merges tokens with identical compiled effects before runtime contexts are bound, so groups are reused across stacks and environments even when concrete successors differ. Objective-specific score aggregation and token restoration provably preserve both the Viterbi optimum and the token-labeled path distribution of ungrouped inference. Across three benchmarks and two DLM families, DConstrain compiles 100% of BFCL schemas under arbitrary property orders, whereas an optimized finite-state baseline compiles only 71.30-85.81%. Under arbitrary property order, it reduces BFCL end-to-end decoding latency by 6.65-46.26 times with 100% schema validity and raises DiffusionGemma's BFCL accuracy from 37.27% under fixed order to 81.56%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Just as different languages express the same concept through different words, neural networks express similar functions through incompatible coordinate systems. Different architectures, random initializations, and optimization trajectories produce incompatible parameterizations in extrinsic coordinates, obscuring functional relationships between independently trained models. When models independently solve similar tasks on related data, do their learned representations share exploitable structure despite incompatible coordinates? This thesis shows that semantic correspondence is a fundamental primitive revealing shared geometric structure beneath incompatible parameterizations. By identifying which elements refer to the same semantic content, correspondence breaks the symmetry of arbitrary implementation choices, anchoring incompatible systems to a shared semantic frame and revealing the intrinsic geometry that enables zero-shot transformation between independently trained models.
  Building on this principle, fourteen contributions explore correspondence-based alignment across zero-shot model stitching, cross-modal translation, policy transfer, activation steering, and neural decoding. They span domains (vision, language, reinforcement learning, neuroscience), architectures (CNNs, Transformers, graph networks), and scales (from small supervised models to billion-parameter generative systems). Each exploits different geometric properties (angular, metric, spectral, sparse, or distributional), yet all instantiate the same principle. It extends beyond artificial systems: fMRI data show that different human brains processing identical stimuli exhibit alignable geometric structure.
  By revealing intrinsic geometric structure beneath extrinsic parameterization, semantic correspondence transforms model isolation into interoperability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Driving vision-language-action (VLA) models bring an appealing form of explainability to a safety-critical domain, since a system that can state the reasons for its maneuvers offers the transparency that deployment and public trust in autonomous driving demand. These models read the world into language before they act on it, describing the scene and reasoning over it in text that ranges from chain-of-thought to multi-stage question answering on the way to a trajectory. That framing quietly assumes two separable properties, namely that the trajectory follows the generated reasoning and that the generated reasoning tracks the causes that actually produce the trajectory. To thoroughly investigate these properties, we apply causal, counterfactual interventions to four driving VLAs across different benchmarks. First, we perturb the models' own reasoning and show that altering its meaning produces small trajectory changes relative to blanking the same models' camera inputs, a pattern that persists across benchmarks and model architectures. We then construct scene-level counterfactuals through photorealistic removal of key objects named in the models' reasoning and analyze whether the generated reasoning and trajectories track these changes. Overall, our findings indicate that the reasoning these models present to account for their resulting driving behavior is weakly coupled to that behavior itself, pointing to limits on the explainability these systems are intended to provide.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Soil moisture and temperature govern plant-water availability and microbial activity, making them key indicators for evaluating land-restoration efforts on degraded landscapes. However, the physical and data-driven models normally used to forecast them both need information that a newly instrumented restoration site does not yet have: hydraulic parameters for the former and months of per-site training data for the latter. We evaluate TimesFM 1, a 500M-parameter time-series foundation model, zero-shot on 15-minute telemetry from 27 sensor stations across three land-management regimes (Control, Treatment, Scald) at a semi-arid restoration site, forecasting 5 cm and 30 cm soil moisture and enclosure temperature at seven lead times from 1 h to 168 h. Using gap-aware rolling-origin evaluation across sensors, we find that TimesFM beats the best rule-based statistical forecasting baselines (persistence, seasonal-naive, or 7-day climatology) by 21-76% at sub-daily lead times but loses by 6-79% beyond a day, a pattern explained by a diurnal-alignment mechanism that holds for both soil-moisture depths and temperature. A CatBoost baseline trained walk-forward on the same origins with full history and weather covariates never wins overall, and we show that its weakness is partly a cold-start effect specific to short deployments. We additionally quantify the inference cost of zero-shot forecasting on commodity CPU hardware. Our results highlight a practical trade-off between zero-shot forecasting capability, predictive performance, and computational efficiency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Personal agents need to draw on long-term interaction history to retrieve and apply external knowledge suited to a user's current request. Even an answer grounded in relevant documents can be unsuitable when it overlooks the user's circumstances. This creates two connected challenges: using historical conditions to identify the knowledge needed for a new request, and applying that knowledge to the user's situation. Existing benchmarks advance memory recall, personalized retrieval, and memory-guided action, while the joint evaluation of these two challenges remains underexplored. We introduce ColaBench, a synthetic benchmark in health and daily living, with 349 tasks across 10 Profiles and a knowledge base of 13,033 documents. Its Evidence Graph connects personal conditions to their disclosures across public interaction sessions, records prior document exposure, and links answer requirements to personal and newly acquired document evidence. We also introduce MemKB, which uses state summaries to inform queries and rule application, and memory-document links to retrieve knowledge associated with the user's history. MemKB achieves 86.25% Accuracy with an average of 4.07 interaction rounds per task. Task-level analysis finds larger gains on tasks integrating information across multiple historical Facts; error analysis further identifies unsupported personal assumptions and rule misapplication as obstacles to using retrieved evidence. ColaBench provides traceable tasks and an evaluation framework for studying how memory guides knowledge acquisition and rule application.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Existing agricultural advisories to farmers suffer from several limitations: (a) remain largely expert-driven, (b) do not adequately account for in-season weather, and (c) do not scale to individual farms. In this paper, our objective is to close these gaps by developing Agri-SCHOLAR (Simulation-Grounded Crop Harvest Optimization with LLM-Guided In-Season Advisory Rollouts). Agri-SCHOLAR is a rolling, in-season advisory framework that issues, at intervals of every few days or every week, a personalized advisory using the following information: soil and crop properties, observed weather to date, short-range forecast of weather, and a season extrapolation of weather based on historical analogs. The novelty and depth of Agri-SCHOLAR lies in each advisory being produced by a closed-loop system which couples multiple LLM agents with a powerful crop simulator (such as APSIM in our case). The simulator crucially serves as an evaluation oracle. In Agri-SCHOLAR, the LLM agents propose agronomically sound and feasible bounds on actionable parameters and an optimizer searches within those bounds against a multi-objective reward spanning yield, resource utilization, and economic feasibility. We conduct a detailed 11-year analysis of the maize crop in two selected locations of South Asia and show that Agri-SCHOLAR consistently outperforms the region's standard packages of practice and satisfactorily addresses the limitations of existing agricultural advisories.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-text image-text alignment is essential for fine-grained scene understanding, yet existing methods mainly focus on extending context length or improving local alignment. We identify an overlooked failure mode, saliency collapse, where models over-rely on text that features visual prominence while underutilizing non-salient context. Consequently, retrieval performance degrades sharply when salient content is reduced, despite that the non-salient context remains informative. To address this, we propose RoSA, a saliency-aware finetuning framework that decomposes image-text pairs into complementary salient and contextual views, enabling joint learning of prominent and contextual semantics. We propose a modality-independent decomposition strategy to combine object-level visual saliency estimation with LLM-based text decomposition to bridge vision and language. We employ auxiliary objectives to explicitly align these views, ensuring robust representation with both salient and contextual features. RoSA greatly improves long-text retrieval and demonstrates strong robustness to saliency collapse across benchmarks. Moreover, our auxiliary objectives are plug-and-play, bringing consistent gains to existing tuning methods. Code and checkpoints are available at: https://github.com/intelligolabs/RoSA

</details>
